========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don't know... my mom made me come. She says I'm not doing anything, and I guess she's right, but I just... I can't. I just can't make myself do anything anymore. I used to do things, you know? I used to knit all the time, I had this huge box of yarn in all these different colors, and I'd make scarves and hats for everyone. And I used to write in my journal every single night, just pages and pages about everything that happened that day, every little thought I had. I even used to go walking around the neighborhood, just to get outside and feel the air. But now... now I just sit in my room. I sit there for hours and hours, and I don't even realize how much time has passed. I wear the same clothes for days because changing just feels like... like climbing a mountain. It's too much effort. And my mom comes in and she asks me what's wrong, she asks me why I'm just sitting there, and I hear her, I really do, but I just... I barely have the energy to even open my mouth to answer her. It's like all the energy has just drained out of my body and my brain, and there's nothing left but this heavy, empty feeling. So I just sit there. And she gets frustrated, but I can't explain it to her because even trying to explain it takes more energy than I have. So here I am, because she drove me here and walked me in, but I don't really know what to say besides that.

Well... it feels like forever, honestly. But if I really think about it... the whole decline, like when things really started going downhill and I started losing all that energy... it’s been going on since I started the community-college program. And it just kept spreading. It just kept getting worse and worse over these last two years and two months, until I just stopped going altogether and ended up just sitting in my room like I do now. It’s just been this long, slow fade since that first week of college, and it just never stopped pulling me down... so yeah, a little over two years of just sinking into this.

I don't really know what you mean by... well, I mean, my mom asks me that too. She comes in and she says, "Are you sad? Are you depressed? Why are you crying?" But I'm not crying. Voices? No... no, nothing like that. I know what you mean, like in movies where people hear things, but I don't get that. It's really quiet in my head. Actually, it's... it's almost too quiet, if that makes sense. Because I remember when I used to think a lot, when I'd have all these thoughts bouncing around, like when I'd write in my journal and I'd have so much to say about what I saw or what I wanted to do or what I was wondering about. But now... it's just really still. 

My mom asked me that once, she asked if I was hearing things because I was just staring at the wall for so long, and she thought maybe I was listening to something. But I wasn't listening to anything. I was just... staring. There wasn't any sound in the room, and there wasn't any sound in my head either. It's just blank. No voices, no sounds, no... no nothing. Just the quiet. And it stays quiet all day, even when the house is empty and the TV is off. I don't hear anything that isn't really there. It's just... just the empty quiet.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... watching me, plotting against me... I mean, I don't know if I'd use those exact words, but... yes, there's definitely something going on. It started back when I was still trying to do those community college classes, the ones I told you about. I was supposed to be doing this whole clinic practicum thing eventually, and I had to use this online school portal to get my assignments and check my messages. And that's when I started noticing it. 

At first, I thought maybe I was just reading too much into it, but then I started really looking closely at the emails from the practicum people and the comments they'd leave on the few assignments I did manage to turn in. They were changing little words. Just... tiny wording changes. Like, instead of saying "Please review this chapter," they'd say "You must review this chapter," or they'd use a specific font for one sentence and a different one for another. And I just knew. I knew what they were doing. They were using those little changes to mark me. They were sending signals to each other, like a code, marking me as someone they absolutely do not want in healthcare. 

I tried to show my mom. I printed out the emails and I highlighted the words they changed, and I laid them all out on the kitchen table to show her the pattern. I explained how the practicum people were coordinating through the portal to push me out. But she just looked at them and sighed, and she told me they were just regular messages. She said, "Honey, it's just an automated email from the syllabus," or "They're just grading your paper." But she doesn't see it. She doesn't understand that they know something about me, and they're making sure I never get into a clinic. They don't want me there, and they're making sure everyone else in the program knows it too through those little coded messages. So... yeah, I guess that's sort of what you're asking about. Even though I'm not going to classes anymore, I still think about those emails and how they marked me.

Put into my head? Or taken out? No... no, I don't think so. I mean, my thoughts are my own, even if there aren't very many of them anymore. Like I said, it's mostly just quiet up there now. It's not like someone is reaching in and dropping ideas into my brain or pulling them out like... like files from a cabinet. 

I remember reading about things like that once, a long time ago, maybe in a psychology book I picked up from the library when I was still trying to figure out what I wanted to study. I used to read a lot, you know. I'd sit for hours and just absorb information, and my head would be so full of all these concepts and theories. But nobody was putting them there, I was just reading. And now, even though I don't read much, and the thoughts have kind of... dried up, I guess you could say... the emptiness is mine. 

It's not that someone else took the thoughts away, either. It's more like they just slowly faded out on their own. Like a battery dying in a toy, and eventually, it just stops moving, stops making noise. That's what happened to my thoughts. They just stopped. Nobody stole them. And nobody is broadcasting them, either. I mean, who would want to listen to nothing? There's nothing to broadcast. Just the flat, empty feeling I was telling you about earlier. So, no, I don't feel like anyone is messing with my thoughts in that way. It's just me in here, and the quiet.

So here I am, because she drove me here and walked me in, but I don't really know what to say besides that.\n\nDoctor: About how long have you been feeling this heavy and drained?\n\nPatient: Well... it feels like forever, honestly. But if I really think about it... the whole decline, like when things really started going downhill and I started losing all that energy... it’s been going on since I started the community-college program. That was... I think about two years and two months ago. \n\nI remember because my mom had helped me pick out all these classes, and I had my schedule all printed out, and I had these brand new notebooks. I was supposed to be doing all these things and going to campus, but... that's when the heavy feeling really started. At first, it was just waking up and feeling like my arms and legs were made of lead, you know? And I'd just lay there looking at the ceiling, thinking about how I needed to get up for class, but I just couldn't make my muscles work. I'd miss one class, and then another, and I'd tell myself I'd go the next day, but the next day was just as heavy. \n\nAnd it just kept spreading. It just kept getting worse and worse over these last two years and two months, until I just stopped going altogether and ended up just sitting in my room like I do now. It’s just been this long, slow fade since that first week of college, and it just never stopped pulling me down... so yeah, a little over two years of just sinking into this.\n\nDoctor: {\"intent\": \"Assess whether the patient experiences a persistently depressed or sad mood to differentiate a depressive disorder from the avolition and affective blunting of simple schizophrenia.\", \"question\": \"During these past two\n\nPatient: I don't really know what you mean by... well, I mean, my mom asks me that too. She comes in and she says, \"Are you sad? Are you depressed? Why are you crying?\" But I'm not crying. I haven't cried in... I don't even know how long. It's not like that. It's not like when my dog died in middle school and I cried so hard my chest hurt and everything felt dark and horrible and I couldn't stop thinking about how much I missed him. That was sad. This isn't... sad. \n\nIt's just... nothing. It's not a feeling at all, really. It's just flat. Like, if you poured water on a table, it just sits there, it doesn't go anywhere. That's what it is. I don't feel happy, I don't feel sad, I don't feel angry when my mom yells at me to get up. I just watch her mouth moving and hear the words, but they don't land anywhere inside me to make a feeling happen. I just feel the heavy part, the tired part, the part where my muscles won't work to get me out of the chair or change my shirt. But my mood? I don't think I have a mood. It's just... empty. Just completely, totally empty. So no, I wouldn't say I feel sad. I just don't feel much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or sounds that other people couldn't hear?\n\nPatient: Voices? No... no, nothing like that. I know what you mean, like in movies where people hear things, but I don't get that. It's really quiet in my head. Actually, it's... it's almost too quiet, if that makes sense. Because I remember when I used to think a lot, when I'd have all these thoughts bouncing around, like when I'd write in my journal and I'd have so much to say about what I saw or what I wanted to do or what I was wondering about. But now... it's just really still. \n\nMy mom asked me that once, she asked if I was hearing things because I was just staring at the wall for so long, and she thought maybe I was listening to something. But I wasn't listening to anything. I was just... staring. There wasn't any sound in the room, and there wasn't any sound in my head either. It's just blank. No voices, no sounds, no... no nothing. Just the quiet. And it stays quiet all day, even when the house is empty and the TV is off. I don't hear anything that isn't really there. It's just... just the empty quiet.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like people were watching you, plotting against you, or that you had special thoughts or messages that others couldn't understand?\n\nPatient: Well... watching me, plotting against me... I mean, I don't know if I'd use those exact words, but... yes, there's definitely something going on. It started back when I was still trying to do those community college classes, the ones I told you about. I was supposed to be doing this whole clinic practicum thing eventually, and I had to use this online school portal to get my assignments and check my messages. Emotions that don't fit? Like laughing when something is sad, or crying for no reason? No... no, I don't really have that happen. It's actually kind of the opposite, really. I don't really react to much of anything anymore. 

I remember when I was in high school, I used to have really big reactions to things. If I was happy, I'd be bouncing off the walls, and if I was sad, I'd cry for hours in my room with the music turned all the way up. I felt everything so strongly back then. But now... it's like that quiet I was telling you about earlier. The quiet in my head kind of spread to the rest of me, to my chest and my face and everything else. So if something sad happens, I don't laugh or anything weird like that. That wouldn't make sense. I just... I just don't do anything at all. 

Like, my mom was watching this movie the other day in the living room, one of those really dramatic ones she loves on the TV where someone always gets sick or loses their house or something terrible happens. And she was sitting on the couch just bawling her eyes out, going through half a box of tissues, just really sobbing. And she looked over at me, probably expecting me to be sad too, or maybe she thought I'd think it was stupid and tease her about it, because I used to do that when I was younger. But I didn't laugh. I didn't cry either. I just sat there in the recliner, looking at the screen, but my face was just completely blank. 

It was the same thing when my aunt called last week to say her dog died. She was so upset on the phone, and my mom was upset, and I remember thinking that I should probably feel sad, because I used to really like that dog. He was this little golden retriever mix, really sweet. But when I heard the news, I didn't smile, and I didn't cry. I just handed the phone back to my mom and went back to my room to sit on my bed. My mom gets frustrated with me sometimes, she asks why I don't care, why my face is always just frozen and still all day long. But it's not that I'm feeling something different on the inside and showing it wrong. There's just nothing there to show. So I definitely don't have those mixed-up reactions you're asking about. I don't think I have the energy to react to anything at all. It's all just still.

I just feel the heavy part, the tired part, the part where my muscles won't work to get me out of the chair or change my shirt. But my mood? I don't think I have a mood. It's just... empty. Just completely, totally empty. So no, I wouldn't say I feel sad. I just don't feel much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or sounds that other people couldn't hear?\n\nPatient: Voices? No... no, nothing like that. I know what you mean, like in movies where people hear things, but I don't get that. It's really quiet in my head. Actually, it's... it's almost too quiet, if that makes sense. Because I remember when I used to think a lot, when I'd have all these thoughts bouncing around, like when I'd write in my journal and I'd have so much to say about what I saw or what I wanted to do or what I was wondering about. But now... it's just really still. \n\nMy mom asked me that once, she asked if I was hearing things because I was just staring at the wall for so long, and she thought maybe I was listening to something. But I wasn't listening to anything. I was just... staring. There wasn't any sound in the room, and there wasn't any sound in my head either. It's just blank. No voices, no sounds, no... no nothing. Just the quiet. And it stays quiet all day, even when the house is empty and the TV is off. I don't hear anything that isn't really there. It's just... just the empty quiet.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like people were watching you, plotting against you, or that you had special thoughts or messages that others couldn't understand?\n\nPatient: Well... watching me, plotting against me... I mean, I don't know if I'd use those exact words, but... yes, there's definitely something going on. It started back when I was still trying to do those community college classes, the ones I told you about. I was supposed to be doing this whole clinic practicum thing eventually, and I had to use this online school portal to get my assignments and check my messages. And that's when I started noticing it. \n\nAt first, I thought maybe I was just reading too much into it, but then I started really looking closely at the emails from the practicum people and the comments they'd leave on the few assignments I did manage to turn in. They were changing little words. Just... tiny wording changes. Like, instead of saying \"Please review this chapter,\" they'd say \"You must review this chapter,\" or they'd use a specific font for one sentence and a different one for another. And I just knew. I knew what they were doing. They were using those little changes to mark me. They were sending signals to each other, like a code, marking me as someone they absolutely do not want in healthcare. \n\nI tried to show my mom. She said, \"Honey, it's just an automated email from the syllabus,\" or \"They're just grading your paper.\" But she doesn't see it. She doesn't understand that they know something about me, and they're making sure I never get into a clinic. They don't want me there, and they're making sure everyone else in the program knows it too through those little coded messages. So... yeah, I guess that's sort of what you're asking about. Even though I'm not going to classes anymore, I still think about those emails and how they marked me.\n\nDoctor: {\"intent\": \"Assess for thought insertion, withdrawal, or broadcasting to distinguish schizophrenia from delusional disorder.\", \"question\": \"Have you ever felt like thoughts were being put into your\n\nPatient: Put into my head? It's not like someone is reaching in and dropping ideas into my brain or pulling them out like... like files from a cabinet. \n\nI remember reading about things like that once, a long time ago, maybe in a psychology book I picked up from the library when I was still trying to figure out what I wanted to study. I used to read a lot, you know. I'd sit for hours and just absorb information, and my head would be so full of all these concepts and theories. But nobody was putting them there, I was just reading. I mean, I don't know if I'd use those exact words, but... yes, there's definitely something going on. It started back when I was still trying to do those community college classes, the ones I told you about. She doesn't understand that they know something about me, and they're making sure I never get into a clinic. I used to read a lot, you know. I'd sit for hours and just absorb information, and my head would be so full of all these concepts and theories. But nobody was putting them there, I was just reading. It's more like they just slowly faded out on their own. Like a battery dying in a toy, and eventually, it just stops moving, stops making noise. That's what happened to my thoughts. It's just me in here, and the quiet.\n\nDoctor: ```json\n{\"intent\": \"Assess for incongruous or inappropriate affect to evaluate whether hebephrenic features are present, which would favor undifferentiated schizophrenia over paranoid schizophrenia.\", \"question\":\n\nPatient: Emotions that don't fit? Like laughing when something is sad, or crying for no reason? No... no, I don't really have that happen. It's actually kind of the opposite, really. I don't really react to much of anything anymore. \n\nI remember when I was in high school, I used to have really big reactions to things. If I was happy, I'd be bouncing off the walls, and if I was sad, I'd cry for hours in my room with the music turned all the way up. I felt everything so strongly back then. But now... it's like that quiet I was telling you about earlier. The quiet in my head kind of spread to the rest of me, to my chest and my face and everything else. So if something sad happens, I don't laugh or anything weird like that. That wouldn't make sense. I just... I just don't do anything at all. \n\nLike, my mom was watching this movie the other day in the living room, one of those really dramatic ones she loves on the TV where someone always gets sick or loses their house or something terrible happens. And she was sitting on the couch just bawling her eyes out, going through half a box of tissues, just really sobbing. And she looked over at me, probably expecting me to be sad too, or maybe she thought I'd think it was stupid and tease her about it, because I used to do that when I was younger. But I didn't laugh. I didn't cry either. I just sat there in the recliner, looking at the screen, but my face was just completely blank. \n\nIt was the same thing when my aunt called last week to say her dog died. She was so upset on the phone, and my mom was upset, and I remember thinking that I should probably feel sad, because I used to really like that dog. He was this little golden retriever mix, really sweet. But when I heard the news, I didn't smile, and I didn't cry. I just handed the phone back to my mom and went back to my room to sit on my bed.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Getting stuck like a statue? Or just moving around for no reason? No... no, it's not like that at all. I mean, I know I just said I sit still a lot, and my face is still, but I'm not actually stuck. It's not like my muscles lock up or I'm trapped in my own body or anything scary like that. I can move perfectly fine if I actually need to. It's just that... well, there's no real reason to move most of the time. 

Like, I spend pretty much the whole day just sitting in that brown recliner we have in the living room, the one by the window, or I'm laying on top of the covers on my bed. And I might stay there for hours and hours, just looking at the wall or staring at the TV if my mom left it on. But if my mom calls me into the kitchen for dinner—well, honestly, usually she just brings it to me on a tray now because she knows I won't get up—but if she *does* tell me I absolutely have to come eat at the table, I can stand up and walk in there. I'm not frozen in place. My body works. It's just... heavy, I guess. Like every movement takes way too much effort, so I just don't do it unless I absolutely have to. 

And I definitely don't do that other thing you're asking about, the pacing or fidgeting or whatever. I remember my uncle used to do that. He couldn't sit still for five minutes, always tapping his foot or walking in circles around our kitchen island when he'd come over for the holidays. It used to drive my mom completely crazy, she'd always tell him he was wearing a hole in the rug and making her dizzy. But I don't do that. Like I said, I don't have the energy for it. I just stay put. So... no, I'm not getting stuck in weird poses or running around the house for no reason. I just... I just sit. Because it's easier to just be quiet and still.

It started back when I was still trying to do those community college classes, the ones I told you about. I was supposed to be doing this whole clinic practicum thing eventually, and I had to use this online school portal to get my assignments and check my messages. And that's when I started noticing it. \n\nAt first, I thought maybe I was just reading too much into it, but then I started really looking closely at the emails from the practicum people and the comments they'd leave on the few assignments I did manage to turn in. They were changing little words. Just... tiny wording changes. Like, instead of saying \"Please review this chapter,\" they'd say \"You must review this chapter,\" or they'd use a specific font for one sentence and a different one for another. And I just knew. I knew what they were doing. They were using those little changes to mark me. They were sending signals to each other, like a code, marking me as someone they absolutely do not want in healthcare. \n\nI tried to show my mom. I printed out the emails and I highlighted the words they changed, and I laid them all out on the kitchen table to show her the pattern. I explained how the practicum people were coordinating through the portal to push me out. But she just looked at them and sighed, and she told me they were just regular messages. She said, \"Honey, it's just an automated email from the syllabus,\" or \"They're just grading your paper.\" But she doesn't see it. She doesn't understand that they know something about me, and they're making sure I never get into a clinic. They don't want me there, and they're making sure everyone else in the program knows it too through those little coded messages. So... yeah, I guess that's sort of what you're asking about. It's not like someone is reaching in and dropping ideas into my brain or pulling them out like... like files from a cabinet. \n\nI remember reading about things like that once, a long time ago, maybe in a psychology book I picked up from the library when I was still trying to figure out what I wanted to study. I used to read a lot, you know. I'd sit for hours and just absorb information, and my head would be so full of all these concepts and theories. But nobody was putting them there, I was just reading. And now, even though I don't read much, and the thoughts have kind of... dried up, I guess you could say... the emptiness is mine. \n\nIt's not that someone else took the thoughts away, either. It's more like they just slowly faded out on their own. Like a battery dying in a toy, and eventually, it just stops moving, stops making noise. That's what happened to my thoughts. They just stopped. Nobody stole them. And nobody is broadcasting them, either. I mean, who would want to listen to nothing? There's nothing to broadcast. Jump around? Like... getting all tangled up or jumping from one thing to another so people can't understand me? No, I don't... I don't really have that problem. Like I was trying to explain before, there just aren't enough thoughts in my head anymore for them to get jumbled up. You can't really have a traffic jam if there aren't any cars on the road, you know? 

I mean, it used to be like that, maybe, a little bit. When I was in high school, before I graduated... well, barely graduated... I remember taking this English class with Mrs. Gable. We had to write these long essays, and I would sit at my laptop at the kitchen table and my brain would just be going a mile a minute. I'd be thinking about the book we were reading, and then that would make me think about a movie I saw, and then a song, and I'd want to put all of it into the paper. She used to write on my drafts in this bright purple pen—she never used red because she said it was too aggressive for students—she'd write that I needed to focus, that I had too many ideas trying to come out at the exact same time and I was losing the point. 

But that was a long time ago. Now... it's just really empty. If a thought does come, it's just one thing, and it moves really, really slow. Like, I might think, "I am thirsty." And that's it. It doesn't connect to anything else. It doesn't make me think about the ocean, or rain, or going to the grocery store with my mom to buy juice. It just sits there all by itself in the quiet. "I am thirsty." And then even that thought just kind of dissolves away because I don't have the energy to get up out of the recliner to go get a glass of water anyway. So, no, they don't race or jump off the tracks or get confusing for people to listen to. There's just not enough going on up there to get confused about. It's just... one slow thing at a time, if anything at all.

I used to knit all the time, I had this huge box of yarn in all these different colors, and I'd make scarves and hats for everyone. And I used to write in my journal every single night, just pages and pages about everything that happened that day, every little thought I had. I even used to go walking around the neighborhood, just to get outside and feel the air. But now... now I just sit in my room. I sit there for hours and hours, and I don't even realize how much time has passed. I wear the same clothes for days because changing just feels like... like climbing a mountain. It's too much effort. And my mom comes in and she asks me what's wrong, she asks me why I'm just sitting there, and I hear her, I really do, but I just... I barely have the energy to even open my mouth to answer her. It's like all the energy has just drained out of my body and my brain, and there's nothing left but this heavy, empty feeling. So I just sit there. And she gets frustrated, but I can't explain it to her because even trying to explain it takes more energy than I have. So here I am, because she drove me here and walked me in, but I don't really know what to say besides that.\n\nDoctor: About how long have you been feeling this heavy and drained?\n\nPatient: Well... it feels like forever, honestly. But if I really think about it... the whole decline, like when things really started going downhill and I started losing all that energy... it’s been going on since I started the community-college program. That was... I think about two years and two months ago. \n\nI remember because my mom had helped me pick out all these classes, and I had my schedule all printed out, and I had these brand new notebooks. I was supposed to be doing all these things and going to campus, but... that's when the heavy feeling really started. At first, it was just waking up and feeling like my arms and legs were made of lead, you know? And I'd just lay there looking at the ceiling, thinking about how I needed to get up for class, but I just couldn't make my muscles work. I'd miss one class, and then another, and I'd tell myself I'd go the next day, but the next day was just as heavy. \n\nAnd it just kept spreading. It just kept getting worse and worse over these last two years and two months, until I just stopped going altogether and ended up just sitting in my room like I do now. It’s just been this long, slow fade since that first week of college, and it just never stopped pulling me down... so yeah, a little over two years of just sinking into this.\n\nDoctor: {\"intent\": \"Assess whether the patient experiences a persistently depressed or sad mood to differentiate a depressive disorder from the avolition and affective blunting of simple schizophrenia.\", \"question\": \"During these past two\n\nPatient: I don't really know what you mean by... well, I mean, my mom asks me that too. She comes in and she says, \"Are you sad? Are you depressed? Why are you crying?\" But I'm not crying. I haven't cried in... I don't even know how long. It's not like that. It's not like when my dog died in middle school and I cried so hard my chest hurt and everything felt dark and horrible and I couldn't stop thinking about how much I missed him. That was sad. This isn't... sad. \n\nIt's just... nothing. It's not a feeling at all, really. It's just flat. Like, if you poured water on a table, it just sits there, it doesn't go anywhere. That's what it is. I don't feel happy, I don't feel sad, I don't feel angry when my mom yells at me to get up. I just watch her mouth moving and hear the words, but they don't land anywhere inside me to make a feeling happen. I just feel the heavy part, the tired part, the part where my muscles won't work to get me out of the chair or change my shirt. But my mood? I don't think I have a mood. It's just... empty. Just completely, totally empty. So no, I wouldn't say I feel sad. I just don't feel much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or sounds that other people couldn't hear?\n\nPatient: Voices? No... no, nothing like that. I know what you mean, like in movies where people hear things, but I don't get that. It's really quiet in my head. Actually, it's... it's almost too quiet, if that makes sense. Because I remember when I used to think a lot, when I'd have all these thoughts bouncing around, like when I'd write in my journal and I'd have so much to say about what I saw or what I wanted to do or what I was wondering about. But now... it's just really still. \n\nMy mom asked me that once, she asked if I was hearing things because I was just staring at the wall for so long, and she thought maybe I was listening to something. But I wasn't listening to anything. I was just... staring. There wasn't any sound in the room, and there wasn't any sound in my head either. It's just blank. No voices, no sounds, no... no nothing. Just the quiet. And it stays quiet all day, even when the house is empty and the TV is off. I don't hear anything that isn't really there. It's just... just the empty quiet.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like people were watching you, plotting against you, or that you had special thoughts or messages that others couldn't understand?\n\nPatient: Well... watching me, plotting against me... I mean, I don't know if I'd use those exact words, but... yes, there's definitely something going on. It started back when I was still trying to do those community college classes, the ones I told you about. I was supposed to be doing this whole clinic practicum thing eventually, and I had to use this online school portal to get my assignments and check my messages. And that's when I started noticing it. \n\nAt first, I thought maybe I was just reading too much into it, but then I started really looking closely at the emails from the practicum people and the comments they'd leave on the few assignments I did manage to turn in. They were changing little words. Just... tiny wording changes. Like, instead of saying \"Please review this chapter,\" they'd say \"You must review this chapter,\" or they'd use a specific font for one sentence and a different one for another. And I just knew. I knew what they were doing. They were using those little changes to mark me. They were sending signals to each other, like a code, marking me as someone they absolutely do not want in healthcare. \n\nI tried to show my mom. I printed out the emails and I highlighted the words they changed, and I laid them all out on the kitchen table to show her the pattern. I explained how the practicum people were coordinating through the portal to push me out. But she just looked at them and sighed, and she told me they were just regular messages. She said, \"Honey, it's just an automated email from the syllabus,\" or \"They're just grading your paper.\" But she doesn't see it. She doesn't understand that they know something about me, and they're making sure I never get into a clinic. They don't want me there, and they're making sure everyone else in the program knows it too through those little coded messages. So... yeah, I guess that's sort of what you're asking about. Even though I'm not going to classes anymore, I still think about those emails and how they marked me.\n\nDoctor: {\"intent\": \"Assess for thought insertion, withdrawal, or broadcasting to distinguish schizophrenia from delusional disorder.\", \"question\": \"Have you ever felt like thoughts were being put into your\n\nPatient: Put into my head? Or taken out? No... no, I don't think so. I mean, my thoughts are my own, even if there aren't very many of them anymore. Like I said, it's mostly just quiet up there now. It's not like someone is reaching in and dropping ideas into my brain or pulling them out like... like files from a cabinet. \n\nI remember reading about things like that once, a long time ago, maybe in a psychology book I picked up from the library when I was still trying to figure out what I wanted to study. I used to read a lot, you know. I'd sit for hours and just absorb information, and my head would be so full of all these concepts and theories. But nobody was putting them there, I was just reading. And now, even though I don't read much, and the thoughts have kind of... dried up, I guess you could say... the emptiness is mine. \n\nIt's not that someone else took the thoughts away, either. It's more like they just slowly faded out on their own. Like a battery dying in a toy, and eventually, it just stops moving, stops making noise. That's what happened to my thoughts. They just stopped. Nobody stole them. And nobody is broadcasting them, either. I mean, who would want to listen to nothing? There's nothing to broadcast. Just the flat, empty feeling I was telling you about earlier. So, no, I don't feel like anyone is messing with my thoughts in that way. It's just me in here, and the quiet.\n\nDoctor: ```json\n{\"intent\": \"Assess for incongruous or inappropriate affect to evaluate whether hebephrenic features are present, which would favor undifferentiated schizophrenia over paranoid schizophrenia.\", \"question\":\n\nPatient: Emotions that don't fit? Like laughing when something is sad, or crying for no reason? No... no, I don't really have that happen. It's actually kind of the opposite, really. I don't really react to much of anything anymore. \n\nI remember when I was in high school, I used to have really big reactions to things. If I was happy, I'd be bouncing off the walls, and if I was sad, I'd cry for hours in my room with the music turned all the way up. I felt everything so strongly back then. But now... it's like that quiet I was telling you about earlier. The quiet in my head kind of spread to the rest of me, to my chest and my face and everything else. So if something sad happens, I don't laugh or anything weird like that. That wouldn't make sense. I just... I just don't do anything at all. \n\nLike, my mom was watching this movie the other day in the living room, one of those really dramatic ones she loves on the TV where someone always gets sick or loses their house or something terrible happens. And she was sitting on the couch just bawling her eyes out, going through half a box of tissues, just really sobbing. And she looked over at me, probably expecting me to be sad too, or maybe she thought I'd think it was stupid and tease her about it, because I used to do that when I was younger. But I didn't laugh. I didn't cry either. I just sat there in the recliner, looking at the screen, but my face was just completely blank. \n\nIt was the same thing when my aunt called last week to say her dog died. She was so upset on the phone, and my mom was upset, and I remember thinking that I should probably feel sad, because I used to really like that dog. He was this little golden retriever mix, really sweet. But when I heard the news, I didn't smile, and I didn't cry. I just handed the phone back to my mom and went back to my room to sit on my bed. My mom gets frustrated with me sometimes, she asks why I don't care, why my face is always just frozen and still all day long. But it's not that I'm feeling something different on the inside and showing it wrong. There's just nothing there to show. So I definitely don't have those mixed-up reactions you're asking about. I don't think I have the energy to react to anything at all. It's all just still.\n\nDoctor: ```json\n{\"intent\": \"Assess for catatonic symptoms such as maintaining rigid postures, stupor, or purposeless motor agitation to determine whether catatonic features are present.\", \"question\n\nPatient: Getting stuck like a statue? And I might stay there for hours and hours, just looking at the wall or staring at the TV if my mom left it on. But if my mom calls me into the kitchen for dinner—well, honestly, usually she just brings it to me on a tray now because she knows I won't get up—but if she *does* tell me I absolutely have to come eat at the table, I can stand up and walk in there. It used to drive my mom completely crazy, she'd always tell him he was wearing a hole in the rug and making her dizzy. But I don't do that. Like I said, I don't have the energy for it. Well... yeah. I mean, it's the only thing that really matters, even when everything else is just still and quiet like I was telling you. Because I just know what they did, and it's always kind of sitting there in the back of my mind. 

When I was taking those college classes—before I stopped going, obviously—I had this clinic practicum thing. And I started noticing it on the school portal. It wasn't obvious at first, it started with just really little things, but then I realized the practicum people were using these really specific, tiny wording changes in their emails to me. And in the assignment comments, too, the ones you have to click a special tab to even see. It was their way of marking me. Like a code, so everyone else in the system would know that I'm someone they absolutely do not want working in healthcare. 

I would sit there and read the same email over and over, looking at where they put the commas or how they phrased a sentence. Like, they wouldn't just say "good job" or "needs work," they would use very specific adjectives that I know they don't use for the other students. Because if you really look at it, it's so clear what they're doing. They were flagging my file. 

And my mom... my mom just doesn't get it at all. She gets so frustrated with me. I've tried to show her. She'll look at the screen on my laptop and sigh and tell me they're just regular messages, just standard automated emails from the school or normal grading rubrics. She'll say, "Honey, it just says your assignment was received, why are you staring at it for an hour?" But she doesn't understand that it's *how* they said it was received. The specific words they chose to let the other instructors know I'm not supposed to be there, that I'm not fit for it. 

So yeah, even though I don't have the energy to do much of anything else, I still know that's happening. I know they marked me. It's just... it's sitting there, in the portal, permanent. And nobody else sees it but me, and I just... I can't make my mom see it no matter how much I try to explain the wording to her. She just doesn't see the pattern, but I know it's there.

No, no, I don't use any of that. I mean, my mom wouldn't even let me if I wanted to, which I don't. She keeps a really close eye on everything in the house. Like, even the Tylenol and the Advil, she keeps them in this little plastic bin on the top shelf of the hallway closet, the one where we keep the extra towels and the spare blankets that smell like cedar. 

And as for, you know, recreational stuff, or drinking... I just don't see the point. When I was in high school, some of the girls I used to eat lunch with—well, they weren't really my friends, but we sat at the same table near the cafeteria doors—they would talk about going to parties on the weekends and drinking those hard seltzers, or sneaking out to smoke behind the bleachers during football games. They'd always come to school on Monday talking about how crazy their weekends were, and how terrible they felt, hungover or whatever. 

But I never did any of that. It always sounded so exhausting, honestly. Getting dressed up, going somewhere loud, having to talk to people, and then putting all those chemicals in your body just to feel weird and out of control. My brain already feels heavy enough, you know? Why would I want to drink something that makes it harder to think, or makes me feel sick the next day? I just stay in my room, or in the recliner. It's safer there. 

And prescription medications... no. I mean, I don't have any prescriptions. I haven't been to a regular doctor in a really long time, not since I had to get my physical for the college enrollment stuff, before everything happened with the practicum. The doctor there just checked my blood pressure and made me read the letters on the chart, and that was it. She didn't give me any pills or anything. So, no, there's nothing like that in my system. Just me, and the quiet, and... well, the emails. That's all.

========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F20.0"], "note": "Patient presents with a prominent systematized persecutory delusion/delusion of reference regarding coded messages from college/practicum instructors marking her to be pushed out of healthcare, persisting for over two years, accompanied by prominent negative symptoms (avolition, severe apathy, emotional blunting/emptiness, loss of initiative). Hallucinations, thought insertion/withdrawal/broadcasting, catatonic features, and formal thought disorder/inappropriate affect were explicitly ruled out, as well as substance use and mood episodes. This clinical picture fits ICD-10 F20.0 (Paranoid schizophrenia).", "is_final": true}

I just don't see the point. When I was in high school, some of the girls I used to eat lunch with—well, they weren't really my friends, but we sat at the same table near the cafeteria doors—they would talk about going to parties on the weekends and drinking those hard seltzers, or sneaking out to smoke behind the bleachers during football games. They'd always come to school on Monday talking about how crazy their weekends were, and how terrible they felt, hungover or whatever. \n\nBut I never did any of that. It always sounded so exhausting, honestly. Getting dressed up, going somewhere loud, having to talk to people, and then putting all those chemicals in your body just to feel weird and out of control. My brain already feels heavy enough, you know? Why would I want to drink something that makes it harder to think, or makes me feel sick the next day? I just stay in my room, or in the recliner. It's safer there. \n\nAnd prescription medications... no. I mean, I don't have any prescriptions. I haven't been to a regular doctor in a really long time, not since I had to get my physical for the college enrollment stuff, before everything happened with the practicum. The doctor there just checked my blood pressure and made me read the letters on the chart, and that was it. She didn't give me any pills or anything. So, no, there's nothing like that in my system. Just me, and the quiet, and... well, the emails. That's all."
  }
]
